During some stretches she would respond by throwing herself into work and home projects—volunteering for extra cases, redesigning lab protocols, and planning elaborate birdwatching trips and garden overhauls—only to be followed weeks later by periods where she could barely keep up with basic household tasks and began quietly neglecting her own medical checkups. About a month before seeking care, after several nights of fragmented sleep while monitoring her husband, she experienced a brief spell of unusually driven productivity at the clinic and at home, which then gave way to a pronounced downturn in mood and motivation; she started calling off from the running club, forgot key steps in quality‑control procedures, and felt increasingly overwhelmed by the contrast between her professional competence and how disorganized she felt managing her husband’s needs. When a colleague noticed her uncharacteristic errors in radiographic imaging and her husband’s cardiologist suggested that her mood swings and exhaustion might be compromising both her health and her caregiving, Barbara agreed—reluctantly—to a psychiatric referral to understand the recurrent pattern of highs and lows that had become impossible to ignore.\n- Chief Complaint: “My sleep has been all over the place for weeks, and I can’t seem to get any real rest no matter how exhausted I feel.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S024 (Depressed_Mood): Lately it feels like someone turned the color down on my whole life; I walk around the clinic and at home feeling hollow and on the verge of tears for no clear reason, like there’s this heavy sadness sitting in my chest that I just can’t shake. \n- S025 (Loss_of_Interest): I used to really look forward to my Saturday bird walks and fussing over my garden beds, but for the past few weeks even the thought of putting on my boots or picking up my binoculars feels pointless, so I just sit inside and let the weeds and the club emails pile up. \n- S026 (Weight_Appetite_Change): I’m usually very structured with my meals, but recently I’ll suddenly realize it’s mid‑afternoon and I haven’t eaten anything but coffee because I just have no appetite, and even when I force something down it tastes like cardboard. \n- S027 (Sleep_Disturbance): Most nights I’m either lying awake for hours listening for my husband’s breathing or jolting up every hour to check his monitor, and then when I finally do sleep I wake up feeling like I’ve been hit by a truck instead of rested. \n- S029 (Fatigue): By the time I get home from the clinic, I’m so drained that even rinsing out the pet carriers or loading the dishwasher feels like climbing a mountain, and I end up collapsing on the couch staring at the TV without really watching it. \n- S030 (Worthlessness_Guilt): When I catch myself making stupid mistakes with the radiographs or forgetting my husband’s medication times, I just keep thinking, ‘You’re a terrible tech and an even worse caregiver; they’d both be better off with someone else who doesn’t screw everything up.’ \n- S032 (Death_Suicide): On the worst days, when I’m driving back from yet another cardiology appointment, I catch myself thinking it would be easier for everyone if I just didn’t wake up tomorrow, and I’ve mentally gone through how I might make it look like an accident, even though that scares me. \n- S033 (Elevated_Irritable_Mood): Right before this crash, I had this spell where I felt oddly upbeat and wired despite barely sleeping, snapping at anyone who got in my way but also talking a mile a minute about new lab protocols and home projects like everything was suddenly urgent and brilliant. \n- S034 (Increased_Activity): During that high stretch, I was buzzing around the clinic starting three different quality‑improvement projects at once, rearranging supply cabinets, volunteering for extra cases, then going home and staying up late reorganizing closets and sketching out a complete garden redesign. \n- S036 (Decreased_Sleep): For about a week there, I was only sleeping three or four hours a night, waking up before the alarm full of ideas and feeling like I didn’t actually need more rest, even though I logically knew it couldn’t be good for me. \n- S040 (Increased_Goal_Activity): I found myself emailing colleagues at all hours with detailed plans for new imaging checklists, signing up for every optional training, and then on the same nights mapping out elaborate birdwatching trips for next year as if I had endless time and energy to follow through. \n- S041 (Painful_Consequence_Behavior): In that revved‑up phase I ordered a bunch of expensive garden equipment and specialty bird feeders online without really checking our budget, and I was driving faster and more aggressively between appointments, telling myself I just needed to “make up time” even though I knew it was risky. \n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: Over the past year, my swings between being overamped and then barely able to function have led to formal write‑ups at the clinic for repeated imaging errors, strained relationships with my running group and close friends because I keep canceling, and me skipping my own medical appointments and basic house upkeep while I struggle to keep my husband’s care on track.\n- duration: Looking back, these cycles of getting revved up and productive and then crashing into long, dark lows have been happening on and off for about a year since my husband’s heart problems really started ramping up.\n- depressive_episode_duration: This current heavy depressive stretch has been hanging over me for close to a year now, with only brief breaks when I felt more wired and driven before sinking back down again.\n\nRules:\n- If the patient can specifically answer based on the profile, set matched=true, list related\n  sections/items in matched_sections, and write a short English strategy in answer_strategy\n  on what tone and content the patient should reply with.\n- If the question is About a month before seeking care, after several nights of fragmented sleep while monitoring her husband, she experienced a brief spell of unusually driven productivity at the clinic and at home, which then gave way to a pronounced downturn in mood and motivation; she started calling off from the running club, forgot key steps in quality‑control procedures, and felt increasingly overwhelmed by the contrast between her professional competence and how disorganized she felt managing her husband’s needs. When a colleague noticed her uncharacteristic errors in radiographic imaging and her husband’s cardiologist suggested that her mood swings and exhaustion might be compromising both her health and her like I’ve been hit by a truck instead of rested. \n- S029 (Fatigue): By the time I get home from the clinic, I’m so drained that even rinsing out the pet carriers or loading the dishwasher feels like climbing a mountain, and I end up collapsing on the couch staring at the TV without really watching it. \n- S030 (Worthlessness_Guilt): When I catch myself making stupid mistakes with the radiographs or forgetting my husband’s medication times, I just keep thinking, ‘You’re a terrible tech and an even worse caregiver; they’d both be better off with someone else who doesn’t screw everything up.’ and I’ve mentally gone through how I might make it look like an accident, even though that scares me. \n- S033 (Elevated_Irritable_Mood): Right before this crash, I had this spell where I felt oddly upbeat and wired despite barely sleeping, snapping at anyone who got in my way but also talking a mile a minute about new lab protocols and home projects like everything was suddenly urgent and brilliant. \n- S034 (Increased_Activity): During that high stretch, I was buzzing around the clinic starting three different quality‑improvement projects at once, rearranging supply cabinets, volunteering for extra cases, then going home and staying up late reorganizing closets and sketching out a complete garden redesign. \n- S036 (Decreased_Sleep): For about a week there, I was only sleeping three or four hours a night, waking up before the alarm full of ideas and feeling like I didn’t actually need more rest, even though I logically knew it couldn’t be good for me. \n- S040 (Increased_Goal_Activity): I found myself emailing colleagues at all hours with detailed plans for new imaging checklists, signing up for every optional training, and then on the same nights mapping out elaborate birdwatching trips for next year as if I had endless time and energy to follow through. \n- S041 (Painful_Consequence_Behavior): In that revved‑up phase I ordered a bunch of expensive garden equipment and specialty bird feeders online without really checking our budget, and I was driving faster and more aggressively between appointments, telling myself I just needed to “make up time” even though I knew it was risky. \n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: Over the past year, my swings between being overamped and then barely able to function have led basic house upkeep while I struggle to keep my husband’s care on track.\n- duration: Looking back, these cycles of getting revved up and productive and then crashing into long, dark lows have been About a month before seeking care, after several nights of fragmented sleep while monitoring her husband, she experienced a brief spell of unusually driven productivity at the clinic and at home, which then gave way to a pronounced downturn in mood and motivation; she started calling off from the running club, forgot key steps in quality‑control procedures, and felt increasingly overwhelmed by the contrast between her professional competence and how disorganized she felt managing her husband’s needs. out elaborate birdwatching trips for next year as if I had endless time and energy to follow through. \n- S041 (Painful_Consequence_Behavior): In that revved‑up phase I ordered a bunch of expensive garden equipment and specialty bird feeders online without really checking our budget, and I was driving faster and more aggressively between appointments, telling myself I just needed to “make up time” even though I knew it was risky. \n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: Over the past year, my swings between being overamped and then barely able to function have led close to a year now, with only brief breaks when I felt more wired and driven before sinking back down again.\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief Have you been finding it harder than usual to concentrate, focus, or make everyday decisions?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've been struggling to focus and keep things straight, and I feel completely disorganized. It's led to me making mistakes like forgetting quality-control procedures and making errors with radiographs at the clinic, and I've even missed some of my husband's medication times. It makes me feel terrible because I'm usually so meticulous with everything.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned feeling terrible about these mistakes. Have you also been experiencing strong feelings of worthlessness or blaming yourself excessively for things?\n\nPatient: Yes, I feel completely worthless and blame myself constantly. When I make mistakes with the radiographs at work or forget my husband's medication times, I keep thinking I'm a terrible vet tech and an even worse caregiver. I honestly feel like everyone would be better off with someone else who doesn't screw everything up.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period like this in the past where you felt this same level of sadness and exhaustion, or is this the first time you've felt this way?\n\nPatient: No, this isn't the first time. These cycles of crashing into deep sadness and then getting highly revved up have been happening on and off for about a year, ever since my husband's heart problems worsened. This current heavy stretch has been dragging on for close to a year now, with only brief breaks where I felt wired.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you felt \"wired\" or \"highly revved up,\" did you find that you needed much less sleep than usual but still had a lot of energy, or that your thoughts were racing?\n\nPatient: Yes, during those high periods I would only sleep about three or four hours a night but wake up full of energy, feeling like I didn't need any more rest. During some stretches she would respond by throwing herself into work and home projects—volunteering for extra cases, redesigning lab protocols, and planning elaborate birdwatching trips and garden overhauls—only to be followed weeks later by periods where she could barely keep up with basic household tasks and began quietly neglecting her own medical checkups. About a month before seeking care, after several nights of fragmented sleep while monitoring her husband, she experienced a brief spell of unusually driven productivity at the clinic and at home, which then gave way to a pronounced downturn in mood and motivation; she started calling off from the running club, forgot key steps in quality‑control procedures, and felt increasingly overwhelmed by the contrast between her professional competence and how disorganized she felt managing her husband’s needs. When a colleague noticed her uncharacteristic errors in radiographic imaging and her husband’s cardiologist suggested that her mood swings and exhaustion might be compromising both her health and her caregiving, Barbara agreed—reluctantly—to a psychiatric referral to understand the recurrent pattern of highs and lows that had become impossible to ignore.\n- Chief Complaint: “My sleep has been all I honestly feel like everyone would be better off with someone else who doesn't screw everything up.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period like this in the past where you felt this same level of sadness and exhaustion, or is this the first time you've felt this way?\n\nPatient: No, this isn't the first time. These cycles of crashing into deep sadness and then getting highly revved up have been happening on and off for about a year, ever since my husband's heart problems worsened. This current heavy stretch has been dragging on for close to a year now, with only brief breaks where I felt wired.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you felt \"wired\" or \"highly revved up,\" did you find that you needed much less sleep than usual but still had a lot of energy, or that your thoughts were racing?\n\nPatient: Yes, during those high periods I would only sleep about three or four hours a night but wake up full of energy, feeling like I didn't need any more rest. My mind was absolutely racing with ideas, and I'd find myself talking a mile a minute about new clinic protocols and home projects because everything suddenly felt so urgent and brilliant.\n\nDoctor: When you had those periods of feeling wired and full of energy, how long did they usually last—would they last for just a few days, a week, or even longer?\n\nPatient: Those wired, high-energy periods typically lasted for about a week. During that time, I would feel completely full of energy and only sleep a few hours a night, before eventually crashing back down into a low period.\n\nDoctor: During those weeks when you felt so wired and full of energy, did this behavior cause any major disruptions at work or in your relationships, or did anyone suggest you needed medical help?\n\nPatient: Yes, these swings have caused significant disruptions; at the clinic, my imaging errors actually led to formal write-ups. I've also strained relationships with my friends and running group because I keep canceling on them. It was actually my husband's cardiologist who noticed the toll it was taking and suggested that my mood swings and exhaustion were compromising my health and caregiving, which prompted this referral."
  }
]

This current heavy stretch has been dragging on for close to a year now, with only brief breaks where I felt wired.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you felt \"wired\" or \"highly revved up,\" did you find that you needed much less sleep than usual but still had a lot of energy, or that your thoughts were racing?\n\nPatient: Yes, during those high periods I would only sleep about three or four hours a night but wake up full of energy, feeling like I didn't need any more rest. My mind was absolutely racing with ideas, and I'd find myself talking a mile a minute about new clinic protocols and home projects because everything suddenly felt so urgent and brilliant.\n\nDoctor: When you had those periods of feeling wired and full of energy, how long did they usually last—would they last for just a few days, a week, or even longer?\n\nPatient: Those wired, high-energy periods typically lasted for about a week. During that time, I would feel completely full of energy and only sleep a few hours a night, before eventually crashing back down into a low period.\n\nDoctor: During those weeks when you felt so wired and full of energy, did this behavior cause any major disruptions at work or in your relationships, or did anyone suggest you needed medical help?\n\nPatient: Yes, these swings have caused significant disruptions; at the clinic, my imaging errors actually led to formal write-ups. I've also strained relationships with my friends and running group because I keep canceling on them. It was actually my husband's cardiologist who noticed the toll it was taking and suggested that my mood swings and exhaustion were compromising my health and caregiving, which prompted this referral.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you felt highly revved up and full of energy, did you do anything impulsive or risky that you wouldn't normally do, like spending too much money, taking unusual risks, or making hasty decisions?\n\nPatient: I'm quite embarrassed to admit it, but during those phases I went online and ordered a lot of expensive garden equipment and specialty bird feeders without checking our budget at all. I was also driving much faster and more aggressively than usual between my husband's appointments, justifying it to myself as just \"making up time\" even though I knew it was really risky."
  }
]

I honestly feel like everyone would be better off with someone else who doesn't screw everything up.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period like this in the past where you felt this same level of sadness and exhaustion, or is this the first time you've felt this way?\n\nPatient: No, this isn't the first time. These cycles of crashing into deep sadness and then getting highly revved up have been happening on and off for about a year, ever since my husband's heart problems worsened. This current heavy stretch has been dragging on for close to a year now, with only brief breaks where I felt wired.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you felt \"wired\" or \"highly revved up,\" did you find that you needed much less sleep than usual but still had a lot of energy, or that your thoughts were racing?\n\nPatient: Yes, during those high periods I would only sleep about three or four hours a night but wake up full of energy, feeling like I didn't need any more rest. My mind was absolutely racing with ideas, and I'd find myself talking a mile a minute about new clinic protocols and home projects because everything suddenly felt so urgent and brilliant.\n\nDoctor: When you had those periods of feeling wired and full of energy, how long did they usually last—would they last for just a few days, a week, or even longer?\n\nPatient: Those wired, high-energy periods typically lasted for about a week. During that time, I would feel completely full of energy and only sleep a few hours a night, before eventually crashing back down into a low period.\n\nDoctor: During those weeks when you felt so wired and full of energy, did this behavior cause any major disruptions at work or in your relationships, or did anyone suggest you needed medical help?\n\nPatient: Yes, these swings have caused significant disruptions; at the clinic, my imaging errors actually led to formal write-ups. I've also strained relationships with my friends and running group because I keep canceling on them. It was actually my husband's cardiologist who noticed the toll it was taking and suggested that my mood swings and exhaustion were compromising my health and caregiving, which prompted this referral.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you felt highly revved up and full of energy, did you do anything impulsive or risky that you wouldn't normally do, like spending too much money, taking unusual risks, or making hasty decisions?\n\nPatient: I'm quite embarrassed to admit it, but during those phases I went online and ordered a lot of expensive garden equipment and specialty bird feeders without checking our budget at all. I was also driving much faster and more aggressively than usual between my husband's appointments, justifying it to myself as just \"making up time\" even though I knew it was really risky.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you felt highly revved up, did you ever experience things that weren't there, like hearing voices, or did you ever need to be hospitalized?\n\nPatient: No, I have never experienced hearing voices or seeing things that weren't there. I have also never needed to be hospitalized during any of those high periods."
  }

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort. Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event. Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nNo, I have never experienced hearing voices or seeing things that weren't there. I have also never needed to be hospitalized during any of those high periods.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate.
